RAJYA SABHA 
Monday, the 9th March, 2026 / 18 Phalguna, 1947 (Saka) 
The House met at eleven of the clock, 
MR. CHAIRMAN in the Chair. 
OBITUARY REFERENCES 
MR. CHAIRMAN: Hon. Members, it is with profound sorrow that I refer to the passing 
away of Shriman Mukul Roy, Dr. Janard han Waghmare and Shriman H.K. Dua, 
former Members of this House.  
Shriman Mukul Roy passed away on the 23 rd of February, 2026, at the age of 
71 years. He served as a Member of the Ra jya Sabha representing the State of West 
Bengal for two terms -- from 2006 to 201 2 and from 2012 to 2017. Born on 17 th April, 
1954, at Kanchrapara in North 24 Parganas District of West Bengal, Shriman Mukul 
Roy entered public life at a young age and em erged as prominent political figure in his 
State and at the national level. Shriman Mu kul Roy served as the Minister of Railways 
in the year 2012 and earlier as Minister of State in the Ministries of Shipping and 
Railways from 2009 to 2012 during his second term in this House. In this House, 
Shriman Roy served as Chairman of th e Committee on Transport, Tourism and 
Culture. Shriman Mukul Roy evinced keen intere st in social and cultural activities, and 
was closely associated with several comm unity development initiatives including 
illiteracy eradication programmes. In the pa ssing away of Shriman Mukul Roy, the 
country has lost an experienced parliamentarian and a dedicated public personality.  
Dr. Janardhan Waghmare passed away on the 2 nd of March, 2026, at the age 
of 91 years. He served as a Member of the Rajya Sabha representing the State of 
Maharashtra from April 2008 to April 2014. Born on 11 th of November, 1934, at the 
village Kavtha in Latur District of Ma harashtra, Dr. Waghmare was an eminent 
educationist, social reformer and distinguishe d parliamentarian. A prolific writer, 
Dr. Waghmare authored a number of books in  Marathi, Hindi and English in the field 
of education, literature, social reform and philosophy. His literary contributions earned 
him several prestigious awards including the Maharashtra Bhushan Award and other 
honours from the Government of Maharashtr a. Dr. Waghmare, inspired by the ideals 
of social justice and equality, worked tirelessly for the upliftment of dalits and other 
marginalized communities, and established a village named Rashtriya Seva Gram  in 
the earthquake-affected region of Latur, where families from di fferent castes were 

encouraged to live together in harmony.  In the passing away of Dr. Janardhan 
Waghmare, the country has lost a distin guished educationist, an accomplished 
author and a dedicated parliamentarian.   
Shriman H.K. Dua passed away on the 4 th of March, 2026, at the age of 88 
years. He served as a nominated Memb er of this august House from 18 th November, 
2009 to 17 th November, 2015.  Born on the 1 st of July, 1937, at Sargodha in Punjab, 
Shriman H.K. Dua was an eminent journalis t and political commentator. Educated at 
Punjab University, he had a distinguishe d career in journalism,  spanning several 
decades. Shriman H.K. Dua also rendered di stinguished services in public life. He 
served as Press Advisor to two Prime Mi nisters – Shriman H.D. Devegowda and 
Shriman Atal Bihari Vajpayee – and later as Ambassador of India to Denmark from 
2001 to 2003. During his tenure in th e Rajya Sabha, he served on several 
Parliamentary Committees.  A recipient of the Padma Bhushan, Shriman H. K. Dua, 
was honoured with several prestigious awards for excellence in journalism. His 
contributions to public discourse and to the strengthening of democratic values will be 
remembered with respect and admiration. 
In the passing away of Shriman H. K. Dua Ji, the nation has lost a 
distinguished journalist, a thoughtful public intellectual and an able parliamentarian. 
We deeply mourn the passing away of  Shriman Mukul Roy, Dr. Janardhan 
Waghmare and Shriman H. K. Dua. 
I request Members to rise in their pl aces and observe silence as a mark of 
respect to the memory of the departed. 
(Hon. Members then stood in silence for one minute.) 
MR. CHAIRMAN:  Secretary-General will convey to  the members of the bereaved 
families our sense of profound sorrow and deep sympathy.
FELICITATIONS BY THE CHAIR 
MR. CHAIRMAN: H
on'ble Members, yesterday was a moment of national pride, as 
the Indian cricket team won the prestigious ICC Men's T20 World Cup, defeating New 
Zealand cricket team in the final by a formid able margin at Narendra Modi Stadium in 
Ahmedabad. This victory marks India’ s third T20 World Cup triumph and, 
significantly, it is also a back-to-back title, with India having won the previous edition

as well. The achievement is even more hist oric as India has become the first host 
nation ever to win the ICC Men’s T20 Worl d Cup, making this accomplishment truly 
special for the country. ...(Interruptions)... The team’s outstanding performance 
throughout the tournament has brought imme nse joy and pride to millions of cricket 
lovers across the country. ...(Interruptions)... On behalf of the House, I extend our 
heartiest congratulations to the players, the support staff and all those associated 
with Indian cricket for this outstanding achievement. The House wishes them 
continued success in the years to come.

Suo-moto statement on “The situation in West Asia”

imperative that the sovereignty and territorial  integrity of all States in the region be 
respected.   Given the gravity of the situation,  the Cabinet Committee of Security

briefed on the airstrikes in Iran and the subsequent attacks in several Gulf  
countries. …(Interruptions)…The CCS was concerned over the safety and security of 
the Indian community in the region. It also  focused on the implications for regional 
security and for economic and commercial activities. The Committee was apprised of 
difficulties faced by Indian travelers transiting the region and by students appearing for 
scheduled examinations in these countries. …(Interruptions)… It directed all 
concerned Ministries and Departments to ta ke appropriate measures to respond to

developments and the relevant Ministries are coordinating to ensure effective 
responses. …(Interruptions)…

nations with mounting destruction and deaths. …(Interruptions)… Normal life and

diplomacy and spoke up for an  early end to the conflict.  Hon. Chairman, Sir, I am

confident that the House joins me in ex pressing our sorrow at the loss of

MATTERS RAISED WITH PERMISSION 
Need for permanent conversion of Train No. 09639/09640 Madar (Ajmer)-Rohtak 
Special into a Daily Intercity Superfast Express 
SHRIMATI REKHA SHARMA (Haryana): Thank you hon. Chairman Sir for letting me 
speak on this important issue regardin g Train number 09639/096 40 Madar (Ajmer)-
Rohtak Special presently operated as special service between Rajasthan and 
Haryana. This train provides crucial direct rail connectivity between Ajmer in Rajasthan 
and key districts in Haryana including Rewari, Narnaul, Jhajjar and Rohtak.  It is 
16
[ RAJYA SABHA ]

extensively used by daily commuters like students, employees, traders and pilgrims, 
travelling to prominent religious destinations in Rajasthan.  
However, Sir, due to its operation un der ‘special category’, passengers face 
uncertainty regarding its long-term continuity and reservation facili ty remains limited.  
In the absence of permanen t daily service on this route, passengers are often 
compelled to change multiple trains leadin g to increased travel time, inconvenience 
and financial burden. The consistent dema nd, high occupancy levels and strategic 
inter-State importance clearly justify the conv ersion of this service into a permanent 
daily train.  Therefore, I urge the Go vernment to permanently convert Train 
No.09639/09640 into a Daily Intercity Supe rfast Express with suitable LHB rake 
composition ensuring adequa te General Second Sitting, Sleeper and AC Chair Car 
coaches to meet passengers’ demand.  This  step will, significantly, strengthen 
regional connectivity between Haryana an d Rajasthan, facilitate educational, 
commercial and religious travel and enhan ce railway revenue.  I request the hon. 
Minister of Railways to take immediate and favourable action in this matter.   Thank 
you, Sir.

Concern over theft and smuggling of India’s Cultural Heritage and Antiquities 
DR. MEENAKSHI JAIN (Nominated ):  Sir, I wish to draw attention to the rampant 
smuggling and theft of our national heritage that is posing a real threat.  All former 
colonies have had the painful experience of symbols of their cultural heritage being 
taken to the land of the coloniser.  India is no exception.  Recently, we heard of the 
Piprahwa Relics, the remains of Buddha.  Th ey were unearthed from Uttar Pradesh in 
1898 and a major portion of them have been taken to England by the excavator at that 
site.  After independence, smugglers have posed a serious challenge to our cultural 
heritage.  Icons and images are stolen from  shrines and archaeological sites and sold 
to museums and art galleries abroad.  Smu gglers sometimes also gift these stolen 
items to earn goodwill and protection.  
24
[ RAJYA SABHA ]

 Though UNESCO Convention seeks to prev ent a thriving market in antiquities, 
it cannot be regarded a great success.  Given the money involved, museums and 
galleries are reluctant to retu rn the artefacts.  They demand proof that the artefacts 
were stolen.  In a small number of cases , where the proof was provided, the items 
were returned.  What then constitutes th e proof?  It could be photographs of the 
objects in their original setting, or maybe a temple or an archaeological site.  Given 
that the literary heritage of India is being digitalised, mapping of heritage sites and 
their holdings could be presented as proof of their ownership and mitigate the threat 
to our civilisational heritage.  Stolen arts , stolen images and artefact images are now 
called heritage crimes.  Among the most nota ble images that have returned home is 
the tracking of the journey of a Nataraja from a village in Tamil Nadu to finally England.  
This is called the Simon Norton Nataraja.  He  was the last owner.  I think, given the 
seriousness of the situation, may we request the hon. Minister to initiate mapping of 
heritage sites and their contents?  Thank you, Sir.  
MR. CHAIRMAN: The following hon. Members associated themselves with the matter 
raised by the hon. Member, Dr. Meenak shi Jain - Shri Maharaja Sanajaoba 
Leishemba (Manipur), Shri Sat Paul Sharma (Jammu and Kashmir), Shri Harsh 
Vardhan Shringla (Nominated), Shri Madan Rathore (Rajasthan), Shri Ujjwal Deorao 
Nikam (Nominated), Dr. M. Dhanapal (Tamil Nadu), Shri Kesridevsinh Jhala 
(Gujarat), Shrimati Sumitra Balmik (Mad hya Pradesh), Shri Pradip Kumar Varma 
(Jharkhand), Dr. Medha Vishram Kulkarni  (Maharashtra) and Dr. Kavita Patidar

ORAL ANSWERS TO QUESTIONS 
Special irrigation support to drought-prone areas 
*166.  SHRI MASTHAN RAO YADAV BEEDHA : Will the Minister of JAL SHAKTI be 
pleased to state: 
(a)   whether Government is providin g any special financial assistance for 
development of irrigation projects in drought-prone regions; 
(b)     if so, details of the number of  project proposals received and approved 
for special irrigation projects in dr ought-prone regions during the last 
three years, State-wise and district-wise, particularly for the State of 
Andhra Pradesh; 
 
26
[ RAJYA SABHA ]

 
(c)     the details of funds sanctioned and disbursed for such irrigation projects 
during the said period, district-wise; and 
(d)    the details of the areas brough t under cultivation through these projects 
during the last three years, in the State of Andhra Pradesh, district-
wise? 
THE MINISTER OF JAL SHAKTI (SHRI C R PATIL): (a) to (d) A statement is laid on 
the Table of the House.  
 
Statement 
(a) to (d)  Yes. Pradhan Mantri Krishi Sinchai Yojana (PMKSY) was launched 
during the year 2015-16, with the aim to enhance physical access of water on farm 
and expand cultivable area under assure d irrigation, improve on-farm water use 
efficiency, introduce sustainable water conservation practices etc. 
Ninety-Nine (99) ongoing Major/Medium Irrigation projects (and 7 phases) 
were identified and included under Accelera ted Irrigation Benefits Programme (AIBP) 
component of PMKSY during 2016-17, in consultation with States, to be completed in 
phases. Out of them fifty-nine (59) pr ojects benefit drought prone areas in the 
country, including seven (07) projects bene fitting drought prone areas of Andhra 
Pradesh. 
To address the issue of development of irrigation in water scarce drought 
prone areas in India, special provisions were  made in criteria for selection of projects 
and central funding ratio during implementation of PMKSY during 2021-26.   Eligibility 
criterion of having at least fifty percen t (50%) physical progress for inclusion of 
projects under PMKSY-AIBP relaxed for the projects having more than 50% 
command falling in drought prone area.   Further, the prevailing funding pattern of 25 
(Centre) : 75 (State) for projects in gene ral category states also enhanced to 60 
(Centre) : 40 (State) for projects benefitting drought prone area in proportion to their 
drought prone command area. 
Nineteen (19) major and medium irrigation projects have been taken up for 
funding under PMKSY-AIBP af ter approval of implementation of PMKSY for the 
period of 2021-26. Out of them five  (05) projects benefit drought prone 
[ 09 February, 2026 ]
27

areas.  Fourteen (14) clusters comprising 1731 Surface Minor Irrigation (SMI) projects 
have been included under Har Khet Ko Pani (HKKP) Component of PMKSY during 
2021-26. Out of them three (03) cluste rs comprising 214 SMI schemes benefit 
drought prone regions.  However, no project of Andhra  Pradesh has been included 
under PMKSY-AIBP/ HKKP in last three years. 
In addition, a Special Package scheme for completion of 8 Major and Medium 
and 83 Surface Minor irrigation projects to address agrarian distress in Vidarbha and 
Marathwada and other chronically drought prone areas of rest of Maharashtra has 
also been approved by Government of India in July, 2018. 
State wise, district wise details, central a ssistance provided and irrigation potential 
created through these projects in last three years are given at Annexure.

(b)     if so, the key performance indicators used for such assessment;

38
[ RAJYA SABHA ]

(e)      if so, the details thereof? 
 
THE MINISTER OF STATE IN THE MINISTRY OF MINORITY 
AFFAIRS (SHRI GEORGE KURIAN): (a) to (e) A statement is laid on the Table of the 
House. 
Statement 
(a) A statutory portal, namely the UM EED Central Portal–2025, has been 
developed in accordance with Section 3( ka) of the Unified Waqf Management, 
Empowerment, Efficiency and Developmen t Act, 1995, and wa s launched by the 
Ministry on 06.06.2025 for uploading details of existing waqf and the property 
dedicated to the waqf, which also automa te entire lifecycle of  waqf properties, 
including registration, verification, survey by the Collector, mutation, annual account 
updates, audit, leasing and monitoring,ensuring transparency, efficiency and effective 
management and faster grievance redressal. However, the said portal does not have 
any provision for disbursement of scholarships. 
(b) to (e) does not arise.

168.   [The questioner was absent.]  
Low airport density in the country 
168.    SHRI S NIRANJAN REDDY: Will the Mi nister of CIVIL AVIATION be pleased to 
state:- 
(a)    whether it is a fact that India ha s only 0.11 airports pe r million population, 
compared with 47.35 in the United States and 0.39 in China, as highlighted 
by the Economic Survey 2026-27; 
(b)   if so, whether Government agrees  that such low airport density poses 
constraints on regional connectivity, air passenger growth and logistics 
efficiency; 
(c)   steps taken by Govern ment to address this infr astructure gap, including 
details of new airport projects, greenfield and brownfield expansions and 
timelines thereof; and 
(d)    manner in which public and priv ate investment, including PPP models, is 
being leveraged to expand airport capacity across the country? 
THE MINISTER OF CIVIL AVIATION  (SHRI KINJARAPU RAMMOHAN NAIDU) : (a) to 
(d) A Statement is laid on the Table of the House. 
[ 09 February, 2026 ]
41

Statement 
(a) to (c) At present, there are 164 operational airports in the country. India is 
one of the fastest-growing aviation market s in the world and is currently the third-
largest domestic aviation market. In or der to address the growing demand and 
emerging challenges in the sector, the Go vernment of India ha s undertaken several 
initiatives, the major ones being as under: 
(i)    The National Civil Aviation Policy was issued by the Ministry of Civil Aviation in 
2016 with the objective of creating a conducive ecosystem for the overall 
growth of the aviation sector in a harmonised manner, covering all sub-
sectors including airlines, airports, Maintenance, Repa ir and Overhaul 
(MRO), and Flying Training Organisations (FTOs). 
(ii)  The Government has launched the Regional Connectivity Scheme (RCS)-
UDAN scheme in October, 2016 to facilitate / stimulate regional air 
connectivity by reviving unserved and underserved airports in Tier-2 & Tier-3 
cities, for making air travel affordable to the masses. Since the 
commencement of the RCS-UDAN scheme, a total of 663 routes have been 
operationalised, connecting 95 unserve d and underserved aerodromes. To 
further enhance regional connectivity,  the Government has announced the 
modified UDAN Scheme to connect 120 new destinations across the country, 
catering to 4 crore passengers in the next 10 years. 
(iii)   Under Greenfield Airports (GFA) Policy, 2008, Government of India (GoI) 
has accorded "InPrinciple'' approval for setting up of 24 Greenfield Airports 
across the country. Out of these, 13 Gree nfield airports at Durgapur, Shirdi, 
Kannur, Pakyong, Kalaburagi, Orvakal (Kurnool), Sindhudurg, Kushinagar, 
Itanagar, Mopa, Shivamogga, Rajkot (Hir asar) and Navi Mumbai have been 
operationalised. 
(iv)  The total passenger handling capacity of all the operational airports in the 
country has increased from approx. 200 million passengers per annum 
(MPPA) in 2014 to more than 570 MPPA at present. 
Further, the expansion and modernisation of airports is a continuous process 
undertaken by Airports Authority of India and other airport operators from time to 
time, depending upon passenger demand forecasts, operational requirements, and 
demand from airlines, availability of land, financial viability, statutory and regulatory 
42
[ RAJYA SABHA ]

clearances and other site-specific factors. A ccordingly, timelines for completion vary 
depending on these factors.  
(d) Public Private Partnership (PPP)  models are being leveraged for 
expansion of airport capacity in the country for both brownfield as well as greenfield 
airports. In the case of brownf ield airports, AAI has leased out eight of its airports 
namely, Delhi, Mumbai, Lucknow, Ahmedaba d, Mangaluru, Jaipur, Guwahati and 
Thiruvananthapuram for operation, mana gement and development on a long-term 
lease basis. The Concessionaire of these airports are obligated to implement the 
Master Plan and Development Works as stip ulated in the Operation, Management & 
Development Agreement (OMDA) and Concession Agreement, as applicable. 
Further, eleven airports of AAI, comprising fi ve major airports clubbed with six smaller 
airports, have also been identified for their operation, management and development 
under Public Private Partnership (PPP). In  addition, the development of greenfield 
airports such as Navi Mumbai Internationa l Airport and Noida Inte rnational Airport is 
also being done through PPP mode. 
To improve the airport infrastructure in the country, AAI and PPP Partners have 
incurred CAPEX of more than Rs. 96000 crore during FY 2019-20 to FY 2024-25.

SHRI KINJARAPU RAMMOHAN NAIDU: Sir, 
regarding the airport development in 
Telangana, let me just start by saying that  the only airport that is functional in 
Telangana is the Hyderabad Airport.   Then, back in the days when  Chandrababu 
Naidu Garu was Chief Minister of erstwhile Andhra Pradesh,  he envisioned to have a 
much bigger airport and th e first Greenfield Airport of  its kind. That is when 
Shamshabad Airport came into  place and that became the major operational airport 
for Telangana and the city of Hyderabad. And,  for a long time! It is because this area 
of Telangana is also quite huge and the demand was there from multiple areas that we 
need to have more airports in Telangana also. Let us start with the Mamnoor airport 
which is in Warangal. Mamnoor airport is one of the oldest airports in the country. It 
was the pre-British era.  This was a very solid operational airport until 1983--if I am 
not wrong about the date or th e year--there was Vayudoot also operating.  It is with 
the Airport Authority of India and we have approximately 700 acres of land. But, with 
the orientation of the area available with us, we can only have a length of around 1800 
meters. We had a vision that if we want to  make the airport operational, we want to 
have a much bigger runway so that wide-b ody aircraft can also have international 
flights. We have requested the State Gove rnment that an additional 250 acres is 
[ 09 February, 2026 ]
45

required. I would like to thank the State Gove rnment also. They have taken it up on a 
very war-footing basis and they have given us land. Now, the Airport Authority of 
India is ready with the planning stage. Procedural due diligence is happening right 
now and, very soon, we want to lay the foundation stone. After that, we are seeing a 
time of two years until which the Mamnoor Airp ort is going to be operational. It is 
going to be a big airport where wide-body airc rafts are also going to operate and it is 
up to 140 Kilometer nautical distance from Shamshabad Airport and the NOC has also 
been given for that. So, there seems to be no other limitations for having operations 
from the Mamnoor Airport. Other than that, there are two other locations that we are 
actively looking, in correspondence wi th the State Government. One is the 
Kothagudem area. In Kothagudem, one site was already identified and when we went 
for site clearance, there we re a couple of limitations and obstacles. There were two 
hills on one side of the site that was mentioned. So, an alternate site was also 
proposed. For the site clearance process,  some weather data was requested from 
the State Government which is still pendin g.  We are still re questing the State 
Government to send that data. Once that is given, once the feasibility study is 
properly done, once it looks to tally safe for us to operate from that field, then, site 
clearance will be given for the State to take up that project. The third site that I would 
like to mention is the Adilabad airport. With regard to Adilabad airport, there was once 
a defence field. We have been consulting th e Ministry of Defence also so that we can 
start civil aviation operations there. They  have also agreed to give in-principle 
approval so that we can have a much bigge r area field there.  Additional land is 
required at the Adilabad airport. The State has been requested to give the additional 
land that is required. Once that is there, Adilabad also will be looked into. Likewise, at 
Peddapalli also, there seems to be a requirement. We are exploring the idea of having 
more airports in Telangana and it is becoming a reality also. Mamnoor, definitely, is a 
reality now. With regard to the other airfields,  as I have said that land has to be given 
by the State Government for us to start the project, the State Government has been 
requested to provide that land. Once the info rmation and land is provided to us, we 
will take the projects forward.

Utilization of funds under Smart Cities Mission 
*169. DR. V. SIVADASAN: Will the Minister of HOUSING AND URBAN AFFAIRS be 
pleased to state: 
(a) the total amount of funds allocated by the Central Government under the 
Smart Cities Mission (SCM) since its inception, year-wise; 
 
(b) the amount of funds, if any, that have remained unutilised or lapsed under 
the Mission, year-wise, along with the reasons therefor; 
 
(c) the total number of cities selected under the SCM and the names of such 
cities, State/UT-wise; and 
 
(d) the number of Smart Cities in which pr ojects have been fully completed, 
the number of cities where projects are near completion and the number of 
cities where projects are still pending? 
THE MINISTER OF HOUSING AND URBAN AFFAIRS (SHRI MANOHAR  
LAL): (a) to (d) The Statement is laid on the Table of the House. 
Statement 
 (a) to (d) Under Smart Cities Mission  (SCM), out of Ce ntral Government’s 
allocation of ₹48,000 Cr, the 100 cities selected under the mission have been able 
to claim central financial assistance of ₹47,458 crore (i.e. 99% of the total 
Central share allocation for cities). As reported by the States/ UTs, as on 
01.03.2026, amount of ₹46,326 Cr has been utilized (i.e. 97% of the total Central 
Share released for cities).  
As reported by the States/ UTs as on 01.03.2026, in the 100 cities selected 
under SCM, out of a total 8064 projects amounting to ₹ 1,64,811 Cr, 7,784 projects 
(97% of total projects) amounting to ₹1,56,159 Cr have been completed and the 
remaining 280 projects amounting to ₹8,652 Cr at implementation stage in 68 
cities.Among the 68 cities where projects ar e at the implementation stage, 59 cities 
have achieved more than 80% financial pr ogress, while 6 cities have achieved 
financial progress between 50 %  to 80%. In the remaining 3 cities, financial 
progress is below 50%. Additionally, 32 cities have completed all their projects. 
State/UT-wise Central Financial Assist ance claimed & utilized by 100 Smart 
Cities and status of projects since inception of the Mission is at Annexure.

MR. CHAIRMAN :  Now, Q. No. 170.  The questioner is not present. Now,

170.   [The questioner was absent.]  
Safety of air operations and DGCA oversight 
170.    SHRI A. D. SINGH: Will the Minister of CIVIL AVIATION be pleased to state:                       
 (a)    the number of air safety incidents reported during the last three years and major 
causes leading to such incidents; 
(b)   whether the Directorate General of Civil Aviation (DGCA) faces shortage of 
trained technical manpower; and 
(c)   steps taken by Government to streng then inspection, certification and flight 
safety oversight systems? 
THE MINISTER OF CIVIL AVIATION  (SHRI KINJARAPU RAMMOHAN NAIDU) : (a) to 
(c) A statement is laid on the table of house. 
Statement 
(a) From 2023 to 2025, 40 incidents invo lving aircraft from scheduled airlines, 
non-scheduled operators, private operator s, and state governments were recorded. 
The investigation on these incidents are carr ied out under Rule 13 (1) of the Aircraft 
(Investigation of Accidents and Incidents) Rules. The majority of incidents occurred 
due to System component failure, pilot handling, non-adherence to Standard 
Operating procedures (SOPs), loss of situational awareness and breach of standard 
separation between aircraft. 
(b) The total number of sanctioned post s of Group 'A' Tec hnical Officers in 
Directorate General of Civil Aviation (DGCA)  is 1063. Out of 1063 posts, 426 posts 
have been created during the period 2022 to 2024 keep ing in view of future expansion 
of civil aviation as part of restructuring of DGCA. 
In last few months, following steps have  been taken to strengthen technical 
manpower in DGCA: 
(i)   22 Group 'A' Technical Officers and 14  FOIs (total 36 officers) have joined 
DGCA since June, 2025. 
(ii)    Offers of appointment have been issued to 106 candidates. 
[ 09 February, 2026 ]
55

(iii)  Offers to other selected 58 candidates are under scrutiny and likely to be 
issued. 
(iv)  Replacement of 42 candidates (Air worthiness Officers) from reserve panel 
has been requested from UPSC. 
(v)   DR Proposal for 82 posts of different technical cadres for vacancy year 2026 is 
at various stages of process. 
(vi)   50 Group 'A' Technical officers have been promoted 
(vii)  DPC proposal for 18 posts at various stages (DPC held for 08 and for 10 
posts at various stages). 
      To meet requirements in the inte rregnum, vacancies are managed by recruiting 
persons through short terms contractual hiring. 
The above measures will further enhance the functioning of DGCA. 
(c) DGCA has put in place measures based on technology, operational 
procedures, and regulations to strengthen the safety oversight system. These include 
the following: 
(i)   "Track the Tail" digital service, hosted on the e-Governance for Civil Aviation 
(e-GCA) platform, enables continuous  tracking of aircraft health and 
component performance, enabling early  detection of safety issues and 
strengthens aviation safety standards. 
(ii)  The repetitive Minimum Equipment List (MEL) items and repetitive 
snags/defects are being monitored through "Delay Defect Meetings" and 
"Reliability Meetings" conducted by the concerned organisation under 
supervision of regional DGCA offices followed with periodic high level 
review at DGCA Headquarters. 
(iii)  A random second level "Desk review" of field inspections and 
surveillance/audit findings for their quality and classification is being 
undertaken by senior DGCA officials periodically. 
(iv)  Additionally, General Circular 1/2025 dated 19th J une 2025 has been 
implemented for comprehensive speci al audit to assess aviation on 
ecosystem and strengthen aviation safety architecture. 
(v)  The systematic safety oversight process of DGCA for monitoring the 
compliance of rules and Civil Aviation Requirements (CARs) includes 
56
[ RAJYA SABHA ]

regulatory audits, surveillances inspections, spot checks and special 
audits as per the risk perceived. The Annual Surveillance Plan (ASP) is 
published on DGCA website and each directorate conduct 
surveillance/spot checks in their resp ective technical areas as per the 
ASP. Findings of these inspections ar e followed up with the concerned 
operator for compliance. Further the compliance of the action taken by the 
operator is verified duri ng the audit/surveillance. In case of any violations/ 
non-compliance to regulations detected during audit/surveillance, 
enforcement action including financial penalty is imposed by the DGCA. 
(vi)   Certification of Operators is carried out in accordance with Civil Aviation 
Requirements (CAR), Section 3 - Air Transport, Series C, under Part II 
(for Scheduled Operators) and Part  III (for Non-Sche duled Operators) 
and other relevant CARs following a five phase process consistent with the 
International Civil Aviation Organization (ICAO) guidance.

when they ensure that 100 per cent safety is there when they ar e operating to these 
airports in the West Asia.  And also, the air lines have given special circulars or travel 
advisories to the passengers to ensure whether the connections are happening or 
not, if they are cancelled or not, so that th ere is no confusion in the travel that is

have a very thorough investigative process. Once all the findings ar e in front of them 
after the investigation, they also have a st rict enforcement policy. I would also like to 
let the hon. Member know that the whole safety does not originate locally, but the 
safety comes from Internationa l Civil Aviation Organization. It sits very regularly and 
gives out circulars on safety management and risk assessments.  All these things flow 
from International Civil Aviation Organizati on, and we try to follow them 100 per cent 
without any deviation because aviation ulti mately is not only domestic but also 
international.  So, when it comes to that , very thoroughly observing what ICAO is 
telling us in terms of safety, we prepare our guidelines, programmes and safety plans.  
So, once the ICAO mentions its safety circ ulars, accordingly, we prepare our safety 
plans regarding that and then comes enforcement along with safety inspection and 
investigation. So, this is a thorough proce ss that we have.   But I would like to also 
mention some of the important activities that our DGCA has taken in the recent 
[ 09 February, 2026 ]
59

months  to improve  our standards in a much better way, which is, involvement of 
d i g i t a l  o p e r a t i o n s .    S o ,  w e  h a v e  m a d e  ‘track by tail programme’ where each and 
every aircraft is tracked digitally.  All the safety monitoring which was usually done on 
paper is now tracked digitally to see that all the compliances by the aircraft and the 
airlines are done on time. And, then, th ey have added another layer of inspection 
from the DGCA, which includes not only what  happens at the ground level but up to 
the central headquarters also . So, there is double verifi cation on whatever safety 
inspections that are happening.  On top of that , safety wise,   circulars that are given, 
we are assessing what is already there an d also trying to see how better we can  
implement them on the ground. So, we ar e taking a thorough feedback from the 
existing processes involving all the stakehol ders, like you mentioned,  the airlines, the 
Airport Authority of India, BCAS and also public.  On many aspects, we are looking at 
the public feedback also and trying to integr ate it so that we come out with the best 
safety norms and standards in the country. An d if you look at it globally, ICAO rates 
us as one of the safest countries in the world when it comes to civil aviation travel. But 
we want to try for much better standards and we will keep trying for that.

National SC/ST Hub Scheme 
*171.  SHRI GOLLA BABURAO : Will the Minister of MICRO, SMALL AND MEDIUM 
ENTERPRISES be pleased to state: 
(a) the aims and objectives of Na tional SC-ST Hub Scheme being 
implemented for SC/ST entrepreneurs;

cent from SC/ST Micro and Small Ente rprises under Public Procurement 
Policy for Micro and Small Enterprises; and 
 
(c) if so, the details of percentage of  procurement from SC/ST enterprises 
since implementation of the mandated  4 per cent procurement, with a 
particular reference to the State of Andhra Pradesh? 
THE MINISTER OF MICRO, SMALL AND MEDIUM ENTERPRISES (SHRI JITAN RAM 
MANJHI):  (a) to (c) A statement is laid on the Table of the House. 
 
 
60
[ RAJYA SABHA ]

Statement 
(a) Ministry of Micro, Small and Me dium Enterprises is implementing 
National Scheduled Caste and Scheduled Tribe Hub (NSSH) Scheme since October, 
2016, with the objective to promote entrep reneurship amongst SCs/STs and to fulfill 
the mandated 4% of procurement from SC /ST MSEs under the Public Procurement 
Policy for Micro and Small Enterprises of Government. 
(b) and (c) Yes Sir. The Public Procurement Policy for Micro and Small 
Enterprises (MSEs) Order, 2012 mandates  25% annual procurement from MSEs by 
Central Ministries/Departments/Central Public Sector Enterprises (CPSEs), including 
4% from MSEs owned by SC/ST MSEs. ‘MSME SAMBANDH’ portal launched on 8th 
December, 2017 monitors the procurement by Ministries/Departments and Central 
Public Sector Enterprises (CPSEs) from  MSEs including enterprises owned by 
SC/ST. Procurement data on Sambandh portal is mainta ined category-wise, but not 
State-wise. The share of procurement fr om SC/ST owned MSEs since launch of 
‘MSME SAMBANDH’ portal across the country as on 05.03.2026 is as under: 
Year Procurement from MSEs owned by SC/ST 
(Value: Rs. crore, Share: in percentage) 
2017-18 544.72  (0.48%) 
2018-19 824.71  (0.54%) 
2019-20 691.43  (0.53%) 
2020-21 767.53  (0.55%) 
2021-22 1302.50  (0.79%) 
2022-23 1546.91  (0.89%) 
2023-24 1761.77  (1.03%) 
2024-25 3731.95  (1.73%)

implementing Pradhan Mantri Awas Yojana - Urban (PMAY-U) since 25.06.2015 with 
an aim to provide all weather pucca houses wi th basic civic amenities to all eligible 
urban beneficiaries across the country. Based on the learnings from the experiences 
of implementation of PMAY-U, MoHUA has revamped the scheme and launched 
PMAY-U 2.0 ‘Housing for All’ Mission with effect from 01.09.2024 for implementation 
in urban areas across the country to construc t, purchase and rent a house by 1 crore 
additional eligible beneficiaries at affordable c o s t  i n  n e x t  f i v e  y e a r s .  P M A Y - U  2 . 0  i s  
implemented through four verticals i.e ., Beneficiary Led Construction (BLC), 
Affordable Housing in Part nership (AHP), Affordable Rental Housing (ARH) and 
Interest Subsidy Scheme (ISS). 
As per the Scheme Guidelines of PMAY -U, Central Assistance is released to 
States/Union Territories (UTs) in three instalments of 40%, 40% and 20%.  The 
release of Central Assistance is incumbent upon fulfilment of mandatory compliances 
by the concerned States/UTs viz. submi ssion of Utilisation Certificates (UCs), 
Aadhaar seeding of beneficiaries, Action Taken Report (ATR) on the 
recommendations of Third Party Quality Monitoring Agencies (TPQMA), Geo-tagging 
of progress of construction stages of houses/projects, registration of Affordable 
Housing in Partnership (AHP)/In-Situ Slum Redevelopment (I SSR) projects under 
Real Estate (Regulation and Development) Act, 2016 (RERA), etc. and other 
instructions issued by Ministry of Fina nce. Due installments are released to 
States/UTs based on compliances as per PM AY-U Guidelines. The scheme period of 
[ 09 February, 2026 ]
65

PMAY-U has been extended up to 30 th September 2026 for completion of under-
construction houses and release of fund s through SNA-SPARSH module. At present 
sufficient funds are available under SNA-SPARSH module.  
Based on the project proposals submitted by States/UTs, a total of 125.15 lakh 
houses, including 13.67 lakh under PMAY-U  2 . 0 ,  h a v e  b e e n  s a n c t i o n e d  b y  t h e  
Ministry, so far. Out of which, 116.57 lakh houses have been grounded and 97.30 lakh 
are completed/delivered to the beneficiar ies across the country as on 02.03.2026. 
Central Assistance of 1.77 lakh crore has been released to Stat es/UTs/implementing 
agencies against the total sanctioned Central Assistance of 2.09 lakh crore.  
The timeline for completion of houses/projects varies from State to State and it 
generally takes 12-36 months in different verticals of th e scheme and as per Detailed 
Project Reports (DPRs) of respective projects. The completion timeline for houses 
depends on various factors like availabilit y of encumbrance free land, statutory 
compliances for start of construction, arrangement of funds by beneficiaries, 
availability of State share etc.  
To ensure transparency and accountability, the Mission uses various measures 
in implementation of the scheme. A compre hensive and robust MIS system has been 
developed which helps all stakeholders to seamlessly manage information pertaining 
to physical and financial progress. The MI S is equipped with Geo-tagging features 
and integrated with BHUVAN Portal of Na tional Remote Sensing Centre (NRSC) and 
BHARAT MAP of National Informatics Cent re (NIC) for monitoring the progress of 
construction of houses. MIS has also been integrated with UMANG Mobile App, NITI 
Aayog Dashboard and DBT Bharat Portal for dissemination of information. To ensure 
that benefits go only to the genuine benefi ciaries, various linkages have been placed 
such as UIDAI for Aadhaar validation of beneficiaries and GIS based Central MIS etc. 
The Ministry conducts regular review wi th the States/UTs on progress of the 
scheme including timely submission of co mpliances by States/UTs for subsequent 
release of Central Assistance and its utilization for completion of the sanctioned 
houses within the stipulated timeline. The progress of the scheme can also be 
accessed on the PMAY-U dashboard (https://dashboard.pmay-urban.gov.in).

Emissions, and 171 st in Ecosystem Vitality.  Only in Climate Change Policy, we have

[ RAJYA SABHA ]

 Sir, MoEF’s budget allocation for the year 2026-27 is Rs.4,413 crore which is 
less than 0.1 per cent of the Union Budget. For a Ministry tasked with India's air, 
water, biodiversity, forest, climate change and waste management, this is very small 
and very miniscule. We need to really look at  this. So we need to bring this structural 
change around the investment. With regard  to the knowledge and skill that this 
Department has to possess, certainly, a detailed strategy has to be there. हमȂ 25 साल 
आगे की सोच कर strategy बनानी पड़ेगी।  For that, one of the implementing agencies of 
the Ministry is CPCB. When I see Cent ral Pollution Control Board, it has 
approximately 500 full-time employees for a nation of 1.4 billion people. Comparable 
agencies in developed nations have minimum 10 to 30 times of this capacity relative to 
population. Sir, I think this is very impo rtant and CPCB needs to  be strengthened a 
lot. The Ministry was last conceptually restructured in 2014 when ‘climate change’ 
was added to its name. But no comprehens ive strategic review of its mandates, 
structure or capabilities has been conducted.  This also needs to be completely re-
looked. The Ministry operates in thematic silos--forests versus wildlife versus 
pollution versus climate change--rather than through functional convergence. It lacks 
dedicated verticals for circular economy, just transition, climate finance, critical 
minerals, environmental technologies and su stainable solutions. Because of all these 
things, the Ministry needs a structural transformation and restructuring. I also want to 
say that there are some bottlenecks, federal bottleneck, that is, strong laws and weak 
implementation. India possesses one of the most comprehensive environmental 
legislative frameworks in the developing wo rld—EPA, 1986; Air Act, 1981; Water Act, 
1974; NGT, Biodiversity Act, Forest Cons ervation Act and extensive EPR and waste 
management rules.  We are very good at making laws.  Yet enforcement is 
characterized by a dual-structure paradox. Policy is driven centrally, but enforcement 
is State-led. The State Pollution Control Boards handle thousands of industrial 
consents but are plagued by acute technical  manpower shortage, legacy technology, 
political economy pressures and varying fiscal capacity. Sir, I had worked closely with 
State Boards, Central Board and the Minist ry and I found that the Government of 
India really wants to do, but there is a pressure on the States where States are not 
getting ready to really take this as a missi on. And for that, we really need to ensure 
that the implementation programs must be made mandatory with the money that is 
going to flow from the Government of India. If you do not link these programs with 
those deliverables, it is going to be very diff icult.  An environmental policy is only as 
effective as its enforcement at the muni cipal and State-level. Especially, at 
municipalities, there are a lo t of problems relating to  air pollution, solid waste 
management, water and wastewater. At municipal levels, our system is not effective.

Even though we have strong laws, there is  a will and wish to make them happen from

by 2024, 55 have achieved 20 per cent and almost Rs.11,200 crores allocated by the 
Ministry are yet to be spent and their outcomes are not comfortable.

2030.  E-waste is growing at  21 per cent annually, and plastic waste stands at 3.5 
million tons per annum.  There is a huge oppo rtunity around the circular economy in 
most of these areas.  While an EPR framework exists for plastics, e-waste, batteries 
and tyres, the ecosystem for tracing, verify ing, and recycling waste relies heavily on 
an informal sector that remains largely unr egulated.  India’s waste-recycling informal 
economy employs an estimated 4 million peop le.  Formalizing these networks into a 
scaled and systematic eco-parks industrial setting is critical to moving from paper 
compliance to physical compliance.  For that , Sir, a dedicated national authority for 
circular economy and resource recovery must be established.

SHRI UJJWAL DEORAO NIKAM (Nominated) : Mr. Vice-Chairman, Sir, first of all, I 
thank you very much for giving me the opportunity to participate in the Discussion on 
the Working of the Ministry of Environment, Forest and Climate Change.  Sir, while I 
stand here to make my short submission an d contention, I am reminded of a famous 
often-quoted line from Atharv a veda, which beautifully captures India’s civilizational 
relationship with nature.  It says, "माता  भ
ूिमः पुतर्ोऽहं पृिथËयाः"  which means, “The 
Earth is my mother and I am her son ”.  This simple yet profound thought reflects our 
Indian philosophy.  It must co-exist with th e nature and it should not be destroyed.  
Today, under the leadership of hon. Prime Minister, Shri Naredra Modi, this ancient 
ethos has been translated into modern environmental governance.  Sir, a question 
may be put to me as to what transforma tion we have noticed.  Environmental 
governance in India has undergone a significan t transformation over the past decade.  
I have no hesitation in mentioning that in 2012-13, the allocation for this Ministry was 
about Rs. 2,430 crore.  However, in the cu rrent Financial Year, the allocation stands 
at Rs. 3,759 crore.  There has been substa ntial increase and the real change is not 
merely in numbers.  It is also reflected in the approach.  Sir, the Government has 
consistently maintained that environmental protection and economic development are 
not contradictory goals.  Rather, they are mu tually reinforcing pillars of a sustainable 
106
[ RAJYA SABHA ]

and developed India.  Our traditional wisdom reminds us, “वनं िह जीवनं नृणां, वनं रक्षित 
रिक्षतç।”                                                                               
It means forests sustain life and when we protect forests, they protect us.  In 
recent years, several important steps have been taken in the conservation of forests.  
Today, India has 98 Ramsar Wetland sites comp ared to 26 in the year 2014.  Cities 
like Indore and Udaipur have been recognised as Ramsar Wetland cities, the first in 
the country to receive this distinction. In dia's forests and tree cover now constitute 
over 25 per cent of the geographical area accor ding to the latest forest assessment. 
Our wildlife conservation programmes have also delivered remarkable results.  The 
population of the Bengal tiger has increased to 3,682, accounting for nearly three-
fourths of the world's wild tigers. Protect ed areas across the country have expanded 
substantially, strengthening ecological secu rity. While speaking about the forests, I 
would like to draw your attention to an em ergency issue, that is, protection of urban 
forests. India is urbanising rapidly.  Our ci ties are expanding, infrastructure is growing 
and population density is increasing. In this  context, urban forests are no longer just 
recreational green spaces. They  perform critical ecological  functions. They help 
reduce the urban heat island effect, improv e air quality, conserve biodiversity and 
provide much-needed ecological balance with in cities.  The Government has taken a 
positive step through the Nagar Van Yoja na, under which hundred s of urban forest 
projects have been approved. However, give n the pace of urbanisation, I would like 
to suggest to the hon. Minister, Bhupen der ji Yadav, that the Government may 
consider establishing a National Urba n Forest Conservation and Management 
Authority under the Ministry. Such an auth ority could create a national inventory of 
urban forests, develop scientific management guidelin es, coordinate with State 
Forest Departments and urban local bodies , and ensure long-term protection of 
urban biodiversity.  If citi es are the engines of India' s growth, urban forests must 
remain the lungs of our future. Our hon.  Member, Brij Lal ji, has quoted certain 
incidents which had happened long back duri ng the regime of the other Government. 
He has also quoted how the forest and environment laws were evaded.  No doubt, he 
was a senior police officer.  Being the public prosecutor, I know if we have to maintain 
dignity and sanctity of these areas, then, we have to necessa rily make certain 
changes in the law.  Therefore, I would like to appeal to our hon. Minister, Bhupender 
ji; criminal jurisprudence says that deterren t punishment is not on ly deterrent to every 
criminal, but to give a strong message to the like-minded people. So, I would like to 
say that we have to make certain changes in the environmental law and the forest law 
so that the persons who commit a breach should be severely dealt with by the law.

107

Sir, my next point would be about air pollution. Air pollution, no doubt, is a 
critical area where systematic progress ha s been made. India now has over 1,500 air 
quality monitoring stations, including more than 550 continuous monitoring stations 
across the country.  Under the National Clean Air Programme, many cities have 
reported significant reduction in particulate pollution levels over  recent years. This 
expansion of monitoring infrastructure has improved both transparency and the policy 
response. India's climate action has also gained global recognition.  Between 2005 
and 2020, India reduced its emission intensity by about 36 per cent, demonstrating 
that economic growth can occur along with  responsible climatic action. India's 
renewable energy capacity ha s expanded rapidly and the country is moving steadily 
towards the goal of 500 gigawatts of non-foss il fuel capacity by 2030. Initiatives such 
as Mission LiFE and Lifestyle for the Environm ent, have also brought citizens into the 
climate movement, making sustainability a collective national effort. Institutional 
reforms have also strengthened environmen tal governance. Digital platforms such as 
PARIVESH have improved transparency  in environmental clearance while 
mechanisms like the CAMPA Fu nd utilisation have helped accelerate afforestation 
efforts across the States. Market-based in itiatives like the Green Credit Programme 
are also encouraging greater environm ental responsibility from industry and 
institutions. 
Sir, I come from Maharashtra.  And I ha ve no hesitation to place on record the 
significant environmental progress made by our State under the dynamic leadership of 
the Chief Minister, Shri Devendra Fadnavis , in close coordination with hon. Prime 
Minister Modi ji's vision and the policies of the Go vernment of India.  Maharashtra 
remains one of the leading States in impl ementing the National Clean Air Programme, 
with over 80 per cent utilisation of the Centra l Funds.  It makes it one of the highest 
among major States.  Cities like Nagpur, Nashik, Thane and Pune have recorded 
consistent reductions in PM10 levels compared to 2017-18.  The State has also 
undertaken extensive restorat ion of degraded forest la nd using CAMPA funds, and 
Maharashtra consistently ranks among the top States in CAMPA fund utilisation.  
Under mangrove conservation, Maharashtra has added over 150 sq  km of mangrove 
cover since 2015, as per the FSI data, making it one of the few States showing a 
positive trend.  Importantly, Maharashtra's successful creation of urban green spaces 
such as the rejuvenated Sanjay Gandhi National Park buffer, Navi Mumbai mangroves 
and Nagpur's Gorewada biodiversity pr oject have become national model for 
combining conservation with urban expansion.

Sir, lastly, I must say that environm ental stewardship is not merely an 
administrative responsibility.  It is a moral and constitutional duty.  India's civilisational 
wisdom has always reminded us that prot ection of nature ultimately safeguards 
human life itself.  As we move towards hon. Modi ji's vision of Viksit Bharat 2047, 
ecological sustainability must remain at the heart of our development model.  With 
stronger institutions, better data systems,  wider public participation and continued 
political commitment, I am confident that India can demonstrate to the world that 
development and environmental responsibility  can go hand in hand.  I, therefore, 
support the work of the Ministry and ho pe that the momentum of environmental 
reforms will continue in the years ahead.   Thank you very much, Sir, for giving me this 
opportunity to participate in this discussion.

Estimates of the previous year.  India has also made commendable progress on 
climate commitments.  The country has alread y reduced the emission  intensity of the 
GDP by 36 per cent from 2005 levels.  Renewable energy capacity has crossed 50 per 
cent of the installed power capacity as of December 2025. These achievements 
highlight India's responsible leadership in the global climate disc ourse.  In wildlife 
conservation as well, the Government has continued its commitment through 
programmes such as Project Tiger and Pr oject Elephant.  These developments 
demonstrate that environmental protection is  not being treated as a peripheral issue 
but as a core element of India's developmental framework.                                                              
[ 09 February, 2026 ]

Sir, then, I come to our suggestions. Air pollution remains one of the most 
pressing environmental challenges, particular ly in urban areas. While initiatives such 
as the National Clean Air Programme have  begun to show encouraging results, 
further action is required. There is a st rong case for investing in better field 
infrastructure, technology-enabled monitoring systems and improved welfare 
measures for forest personnel. Stre ngthening livelihoods for forest-based 
communities through sustainable forest produce, eco-tourism and value-addition can 
simultaneously support conservation and rural development.

Prime Minister, Shri Narendra Modi, an d hon. Environmental Minister, Shri 
Bhupender Yadav, has taken significant st eps in strengthening the environmental 
institutions, expanding conservation pr ogrammes and advancing India's global 
climate commitment.

Grants for the Ministry. Jai Telugu Desam! Thank you.

inclusive. India today stands at a crit ical juncture where economic growth and 
environmental protection must move together. The Ministry is interested with 
planning, promoting, coordinating and over seeing policies related to environmental 
protection, forestry, wildlife conservation , pollution control and climate change

mitigation and adaptation. One of the key prio rities of the Ministry is conservation of 
forest and biodiversity sche mes such as Green India Missi on, National Afforestation 
Programme and CAMPA initiatives aimed to expand forest and tree cover, regenerate 
degraded landscapes and strengthen ecosy stem services. These initiatives not only 
protect biodiversity but also generate liv elihood for forest-dependent communities. 
Another important focus area is the prot ection of wildlife and fragile ecosystem. 
Through programs like Project Tiger, Project Elephant and Integrated Development of 
Wildlife Habitats, the Ministry is working to preserve India's rich fauna and maintain

Sir, pollution control and environmental governance remain critical challenges. 
The Ministry, through institutions such as the Central Pollution Control Board, is 
working with States to improve air and water quality, monitor environmental standards 
and manage hazardous substances.  Strengthening this regulatory system is essential 
for protecting public health and ecological su stainability. Air pollution has emerged as 
one of the most serious environmental and public health challenges facing our country 
today. Rapid urbanization, industrial emi ssions, vehicular pollution, construction 
activities and burning of biomass have signif icantly contributed to the deterioration of 
air quality in many cities. Poor air qualit y not only harms the environment, but also 
poses severe risk to the health of millions  of citizens, particularly, children and the 
elderly.  The situation in the capital city of Delhi and NCR region is more alarming.  In 
November, 2025, air quality in the NCR rema ined in the ‘severe’ and ‘severe plus’ 
categories for several days making th e Government to impose emergency 
restrictions.

SPECIAL MENTIONS 
Need to recognise Neurofibromatosis Type 1 (NF1) under the  
National Policy for Rare Diseases 
 
DR. ANIL SUKHDEORAO BONDE (Maharasht ra): Sir, I draw attention of the 
Government to the need for formal recognit ion and systematic care for patients with 
Neurofibromatosis Type-1 (NF1), a rare and complex genetic condition, affecting 
approximately 1 in 3,000 to 5,000 people.  NF1 often begins in childhood and causes 
tumours, bone abnormalities, learning di sabilities and, in many cases, Plexiform 
Neurofibromas (PN), a progressive nerve sheath tumours that can lead to pain, 
disfigurement and life-threatening complications.                     
The psychosocial impact is profound, wi th patients facing stigma, bullying, 
isolation and deep emotional distress.  Despite this, NF1 remains under-recognised in 
India’s health policy, including the National  Policy for Rare Diseases (NPRD).  This 
lack of recognition restricts access to multidisciplinary care, innovative therapies, 
financial assistance and structured rehab ilitation.  Current disability assessment 
124
[ RAJYA SABHA ]

frameworks also fail to capture the invi sible and fluctuating impairments caused by 
NF1.  Scientific progress now offers effect ive and targeted ther apies for inoperable 
Neurofibromatosis.  Yet, without NPRD inclusion, such treatments remain 
inaccessible for most Indian patients.  I urge  the Government to formally include NF1 
under the NPRD to enable standardized care at the Centres of Excellence, treatment 
funding, structured referrals, patient registries and advocacy support.  This is a public 
health and moral imperative.   Thank you, Sir.

Need to protect the integrity of India’s R&D Ecosystem 
DR. K. LAXMAN (Uttar Pradesh): Sir, under the leadership of our hon. Prime 
Minister, India’s innovation ecosystem has s een major progress.  Patent filings have 
increased by over 200 percent in the last decade.  India ha s also steadily improved its 
position in the Global Innovation Index. New India is moving from “brain drain ” to 
“innovation gain ”.  However, there is a serious concern that needs attention. Data 
from the India Patent Office shows a large difference in patent quality. Our IITs and 
IISc maintain grant rates of ov er 40 percent.  But some private universities are filing 
patents on a massive scale, in some cases  more than all IITs combined, yet their 
success rates are only between 0.08 and 1 pe r cent. The problem lies in the incentive 
structure.  With the 80 percent fee reductio n, a patent-filing costs only Rs. 1,600. At 
the same time, Government schemes provide incentives or grants up to Rs. 2 lakhs 
per domestic patent filing meaning a 125-times return.  This may encourage filing low-
quality patents just for financial or ranking benefits.  
To ensure that the Prime Minister’s vision of ‘Anusandhan’ is not diluted, I urge 
the Government to (i) introduce a ‘Success Floor’; (ii) move the bulk of financial 
reimbursements to the ‘Post-Grant’ stage ra ther than the ‘Post-Filing’ stage; (iii) 
direct the National Institutional Ranking Framework (NIRF) to reduce weightage for 
‘Patents Filed’ and significantly increase weightage for ‘Patents Granted’ and ‘Patents 
Licensed’ in institutional rankings.   Let us reward the quality of the invention, not just 
the quantity of applications.  Thank you.                                                
THE VICE-CHAIRMAN (DR. DINESH SHARMA) : The following hon. Members 
associated themselves with the Special Mention made by hon. Member, Dr. K. 
Laxman: Shri Ravi Chandra Vaddiraju (Telangana) and Dr. Bhim Singh (Bihar).
